    *   **Novel Task Definition:** Introduces a significantly more challenging and practically relevant task than simple forgery detection.
    *   **Cross-Modal Watermarking:**  Exploits the redundancy between audio and visual modalities in a creative way.
    *   **Robustness:** Demonstrates strong performance against various manipulations, including those specifically designed to undermine the watermarking process (lip-synchronization).
    *   **Privacy Considerations:**  Addresses ethical concerns by demonstrating the feasibility of training the model on datasets that do not contain sensitive personal information.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results show substantial improvement over existing baselines.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset Diversity:** While the HDTF dataset provides high-quality data, expanding evaluation to more diverse datasets with varying recording conditions and speaker characteristics would further strengthen the results.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The use of INNs can be computationally expensive, which may limit the scalability of the approach for real-time applications.
    *   **Imperceptibility Limitations:** While the watermark's impact on visual quality is addressed with PSNR and SSIM, a more thorough subjective evaluation of imperceptibility (e.g., user studies) could provide further insights. Also, if an adversary knows about the watermark, it is possible to remove it.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of SAVF detection and mitigation. It shifts the focus from simply detecting manipulations to actively restoring authentic content.  The proposed cross-modal watermarking framework could inspire further research into more robust and efficient techniques for embedding and extracting information across different modalities.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's novelty, significance, and strong experimental results warrant a high score. The introduction of the AAR task and the cross-modal watermarking framework represent a valuable contribution to the field. While the paper has some limitations, such as those relating to dataset diversity and computational cost, these are relatively minor in comparison to the overall impact. The ethical considerations regarding privacy are also well-addressed. Although the robustness is good, it is not perfect.

### osmAG-LLM: Zero-Shot Open-Vocabulary Object Navigation via Semantic Maps and Large Language Models Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12753v1)
- **Authors**: Fujing Xie, Sören Schwertfeger, Hermann Blum
- **Abstract**: Recent open-vocabulary robot mapping methods enrich dense geometric maps with pre-trained visual-language features, achieving a high level of detail and guiding robots to find objects specified by open-vocabulary language queries. While the issue of scalability for such approaches has received some attention, another fundamental problem is that high-detail object mapping quickly becomes outdated, as objects get moved around a lot. In this work, we develop a mapping and navigation system for object-goal navigation that, from the ground up, considers the possibilities that a queried object can have moved, or may not be mapped at all. Instead of striving for high-fidelity mapping detail, we consider that the main purpose of a map is to provide environment grounding and context, which we combine with the semantic priors of LLMs to reason about object locations and deploy an active, online approach to navigate to the objects. Through simulated and real-world experiments we find that our approach tends to have higher retrieval success at shorter path lengths for static objects and by far outperforms prior approaches in cases of dynamic or unmapped object queries. We provide our code and dataset at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/osmAG-LLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "osmAG-LLM," a novel approach for zero-shot open-vocabulary object navigation. It combines a lightweight, text-based semantic map (semantic-osmAG, an extension of osmAG) with the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). Unlike methods that rely on high-fidelity 3D maps that quickly become outdated, osmAG-LLM prioritizes a compact, scalable map focusing on environment grounding and context. The system uses LLMs to reason about object locations, even if the object has moved or is unmapped, and deploys an active, online object detection approach. Experiments in both simulated and real-world environments demonstrate the approach's effectiveness, particularly in retrieving dynamic or unmapped objects. The paper claims improved retrieval success at shorter path lengths for static objects and significant outperformance for dynamic and unmapped object queries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in the combination of a text-based, hierarchical map representation (osmAG) with LLM reasoning for open-vocabulary navigation, particularly addressing the challenge of dynamic environments and unmapped objects. While prior works have explored LLMs for navigation and object finding, osmAG-LLM’s map representation and online object detection workflow is unique. The integration of a visual language model to confirm object detections proposed by an open-vocabulary detector is also a beneficial integration.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to create more robust and adaptable robot navigation systems. High-fidelity 3D maps are computationally expensive and brittle, which limits their usability in dynamic real-world scenarios. By leveraging LLMs to reason about object locations within a simplified map structure, the osmAG-LLM approach offers a more scalable and practical solution. The promising performance in retrieving unmapped objects is a valuable contribution towards creating robots that can cope with incomplete or outdated information. The use of OSM is a great design choice for enabling larger-scale navigation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Robustness to Dynamic Environments:** The key strength of the paper is its focus on handling dynamic and unmapped objects, a significant limitation of many current navigation systems.
    *   **Scalability:** The compact, text-based map representation makes the system more scalable than those relying on dense 3D models. The ability to work well with sparsely mapped environments increases the practical applicability of the system.
    *   **Use of Pre-trained Models:** The system leverages existing LLMs and VLMs rather than requiring extensive training on specific environments, improving its generalizability.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments in both simulated and real-world environments provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Open-Source Contribution:** The authors provide their code and dataset, which will promote reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLM Reasoning:** The system's performance depends heavily on the accuracy and reliability of the LLM's reasoning abilities. There might be situations where the LLM’s assumptions are incorrect, leading to suboptimal navigation.
    *   **Map Construction Process:** While the authors mention using LabelMaker and VLMs for map enrichment, the actual map creation process (JOSM, manual editing) still seems manual and potentially time-consuming, limiting scalability. The sparsity of the map also relies on quality of VLM’s performance in annotating objects in the environment. More automation and robust mechanisms for map creation and updating are needed.
    *   **Complexity:** The overall system is a multi-stage pipeline involving a series of different components (Map, LLM, Open Vocabulary Object Detector, and VLM). This increases system complexity and potentially the number of points of failure.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study investigates some of the core design decisions and their impact to the navigation system.
    *   **Failure analysis:** The paper analyses how the real-world system could fail due to object confusion and wrong bounding box proposals.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    While the paper offers a novel and valuable approach to object navigation in dynamic environments, there are some limitations to consider: the potential time and resources associated with creating and validating a high-quality semantic-osmAG. Given these strengths and weaknesses, the impact on the field is substantial, but not transformative.

    Therefore, the score is 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Logit Arithmetic Elicits Long Reasoning Capabilities Without Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12759v1)
- **Authors**: Yunxiang Zhang, Muhammad Khalifa, Lechen Zhang, Xin Liu, Ayoung Lee, Xinliang Frederick Zhang, Farima Fatahi Bayat, Lu Wang
- **Abstract**: Large reasoning models (LRMs) can do complex reasoning via long chain-of-thought (CoT) involving cognitive strategies such as backtracking and self-correction. Recent studies suggest that some models inherently possess these long reasoning abilities, which may be unlocked via extra training. Our work first investigates whether we can elicit such behavior without any training. To this end, we propose a decoding-time approach, ThinkLogit, which utilizes logits arithmetic (Liu et al., 2024) to tune a target large LM for long reasoning using a substantially smaller model as guider. We then show that we can further boost performance by training the guider model with preference optimization over correct/incorrect reasoning pairs sampled from both the target and guider model -- a setup we refer to as ThinkLogit-DPO. Our experiments demonstrate that ThinkLogit and ThinkLogit-DPO achieve a relative improvement in pass@1 by 26% and 29%, respectively, over four mathematical datasets using the Qwen2.5-32B when guided by R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B -- a model 21x smaller. Lastly, we show that ThinkLogit can transfer long reasoning skills acquired through reinforcement learning, improving pass@1 by 13% relative compared to the Qwen2.5-32B base model. Our work presents a computationally-efficient method to elicit long reasoning in large models with minimal or no additional training.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary**

The paper proposes a novel decoding-time approach called THINKLOGIT to elicit long reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs) without additional training. The method uses logit arithmetic to combine the output distributions of a large target LLM and a smaller, more capable "guider" model, effectively transferring the reasoning skills of the smaller model to the larger one. The paper further introduces THINKLOGIT-DPO, which improves the guider model through Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) on preference pairs sampled from the outputs of both the target and guider models.  Experiments demonstrate that THINKLOGIT and THINKLOGIT-DPO achieve significant improvements in mathematical reasoning tasks compared to the base LLM, even approaching the performance of fully fine-tuned models, but with significantly less training data and computational cost. The paper also shows that THINKLOGIT can transfer reasoning skills acquired through reinforcement learning.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using logit arithmetic to transfer reasoning abilities from a smaller "guider" model to a larger target LLM is moderately novel. Logit arithmetic, itself, isn't new (Liu et al., 2024), but its specific application to elicit _long_ reasoning capabilities, especially with the subsequent DPO fine-tuning of the guider, presents a useful innovation. Prior works used logit arithmetic for different purposes like fine-tuning emulation, safety filter overriding, or skill combination, so this paper distinguishes itself in targeting complex reasoning. The idea of improving this setup using DPO by constructing preference pairs capturing complementary strengths of the guide and target models, however, does adds a substantial degree of novelty.

**Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the reasoning capabilities of LLMs in a computationally efficient manner.  Full fine-tuning of large models, especially for long-form reasoning, is extremely expensive. THINKLOGIT offers a compelling alternative, particularly for scenarios where computational resources are limited or rapid deployment is desired. The finding that even a small, RL-trained model can effectively guide a much larger, untrained model is practically very promising. Furthermore, demonstrating that preference learning (DPO) can further refine the guider's influence and boost performance is an important contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Computational Efficiency:** The major strength is the dramatic reduction in training cost compared to full fine-tuning.
*   **Simplicity:** The method is relatively simple to implement and doesn't require modifying the architecture or weights of the target LLM.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides convincing empirical evidence of the effectiveness of THINKLOGIT and THINKLOGIT-DPO across multiple mathematical reasoning datasets.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are well-designed and help to understand the contributions of different components of the approach, especially the choice of preference pairs in DPO.
*   **Transferability:** The demonstrated transfer of RL-acquired reasoning skills is a valuable finding.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is primarily focused on mathematical reasoning. While this is a challenging domain, it would be beneficial to evaluate the method on other reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical reasoning) to assess its generalizability.
*   **Dependence on Guider Model:** The performance of THINKLOGIT is heavily dependent on the quality of the guider model. If the guider is weak or biased, it could negatively impact the performance of the target LLM. This is somewhat mitigated by DPO, but still remains a concern.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The method introduces additional hyperparameters, such as the guidance strength (alpha) and warmup length, which need to be carefully tuned.  The paper could benefit from a more thorough analysis of the sensitivity of the results to these hyperparameters, especially providing a way to adjust these on a case-by-case basis.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a theoretical analysis of why logit arithmetic works in this context.  While the empirical results are strong, a theoretical explanation could provide further insights and guide future research.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. While the paper builds upon existing techniques (logit arithmetic, DPO), it combines them in a novel and effective way to address a significant challenge in the field of LLMs: enabling complex reasoning with limited computational resources. The empirical results are compelling, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. However, the paper has some limitations in terms of scope of evaluation, dependence on the guider model, hyperparameter sensitivity, and lack of theoretical analysis. While not a ground-breaking, paradigm-shifting contribution, THINKLOGIT and THINKLOGIT-DPO are a valuable and practical addition to the toolkit for improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The work is likely to influence future research on computationally efficient reasoning methods.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Think-Before-Draw: Decomposing Emotion Semantics & Fine-Grained Controllable Expressive Talking Head Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12761v1)
- **Authors**: Hanlei Shi, Leyuan Qu, Yu Liu, Di Gao, Yuhua Zheng, Taihao Li
- **Abstract**: Emotional talking-head generation has emerged as a pivotal research area at the intersection of computer vision and multimodal artificial intelligence, with its core value lying in enhancing human-computer interaction through immersive and empathetic engagement.With the advancement of multimodal large language models, the driving signals for emotional talking-head generation has shifted from audio and video to more flexible text. However, current text-driven methods rely on predefined discrete emotion label texts, oversimplifying the dynamic complexity of real facial muscle movements and thus failing to achieve natural emotional expressiveness.This study proposes the Think-Before-Draw framework to address two key challenges: (1) In-depth semantic parsing of emotions--by innovatively introducing Chain-of-Thought (CoT), abstract emotion labels are transformed into physiologically grounded facial muscle movement descriptions, enabling the mapping from high-level semantics to actionable motion features; and (2) Fine-grained expressiveness optimization--inspired by artists' portrait painting process, a progressive guidance denoising strategy is proposed, employing a "global emotion localization--local muscle control" mechanism to refine micro-expression dynamics in generated videos.Our experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance on widely-used benchmarks, including MEAD and HDTF. Additionally, we collected a set of portrait images to evaluate our model's zero-shot generation capability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Think-Before-Draw" (TBD), a novel framework for generating emotionally expressive talking-head videos from text. TBD addresses the limitations of existing text-driven approaches by: 1) deeply parsing emotion semantics using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) to translate abstract emotion labels into physiologically grounded facial muscle movement descriptions, and 2) optimizing expressiveness with a progressive guidance denoising strategy inspired by the artistic process of portrait painting ("global emotion localization-local muscle control"). The paper presents quantitative and qualitative results demonstrating that TBD achieves state-of-the-art performance on MEAD and HDTF datasets, surpassing existing methods in emotional expressiveness, motion naturalness, and user controllability. Additionally, the paper tests zero-shot generation capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in its combined use of CoT for emotional semantic parsing and a progressive denoising strategy for generating talking-head videos. The concept of drawing inspiration from artistic portrait painting is a unique framing.  However, CoT is already an established method, so the real novelty lies in how they apply it to the specific problem. The progressive guidance denoising strategy is also a useful addition.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in improving the realism and controllability of emotionally expressive talking heads. This is a crucial area for enhancing human-computer interaction and creating more immersive virtual experiences. By bridging the semantic gap between text and facial actions, TBD enables finer-grained control over emotional expression. The improvements shown over existing methods, particularly in expressiveness and naturalness, are practically meaningful. The results could lead to better virtual assistants, more engaging virtual characters, and improved communication tools.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined approach.
    *   Innovative combination of CoT and progressive denoising.
    *   Strong quantitative and qualitative results demonstrating improvements over state-of-the-art methods.
    *   Well-designed ablation studies that validate the contribution of individual components.
    *   A user study that further confirms the subjective quality of the generated videos.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the computational cost associated with the CoT module and the progressive denoising strategy. The authors could have compared the runtime and memory requirements of their approach with those of existing methods.
    *   While the paper mentions the use of Wav2Vec for audio encoding and a VAE encoder for image features, the details of these modules are not fully described. A more comprehensive discussion of these components would have been helpful.
    *   The paper's zero-shot generation ability is somewhat limited in scope. It would be more interesting to show generalization across a diverse set of identities and expressions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has a good potential influence on the field.  The TBD framework offers a more principled and effective approach to generating emotionally expressive talking heads. The techniques used, specifically the integration of CoT and progressive denoising, can potentially be applied to other generative tasks that require fine-grained control and nuanced expressiveness. Future research may build upon the TBD framework by exploring adaptive emotion modeling for personalized avatars and real-time synthesis optimizations.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable contribution to the field, the incremental nature of its novelty necessitates a more moderate score. The application of CoT and progressive denoising, while effective, builds upon established techniques. The performance gains are significant, but not revolutionary. The identified weaknesses, such as the lack of detailed computational cost analysis and limited zero-shot generalization, further justify a slightly lower score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Local Representative Token Guided Merging for Text-to-Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12771v1)
- **Authors**: Min-Jeong Lee, Hee-Dong Kim, Seong-Whan Lee
- **Abstract**: Stable diffusion is an outstanding image generation model for text-to-image, but its time-consuming generation process remains a challenge due to the quadratic complexity of attention operations. Recent token merging methods improve efficiency by reducing the number of tokens during attention operations, but often overlook the characteristics of attention-based image generation models, limiting their effectiveness. In this paper, we propose local representative token guided merging (ReToM), a novel token merging strategy applicable to any attention mechanism in image generation. To merge tokens based on various contextual information, ReToM defines local boundaries as windows within attention inputs and adjusts window sizes. Furthermore, we introduce a representative token, which represents the most representative token per window by computing similarity at a specific timestep and selecting the token with the highest average similarity. This approach preserves the most salient local features while minimizing computational overhead. Experimental results show that ReToM achieves a 6.2% improvement in FID and higher CLIP scores compared to the baseline, while maintaining comparable inference time. We empirically demonstrate that ReToM is effective in balancing visual quality and computational efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Local Representative Token Guided Merging (ReToM), a novel token merging strategy for improving the efficiency and quality of text-to-image generation using stable diffusion models. ReToM addresses the quadratic complexity of attention mechanisms by merging tokens within local windows. It adaptively adjusts window sizes based on the U-Net architecture and selects representative tokens within each window using a similarity-based approach. The method also incorporates a similarity computation caching strategy to further reduce computational overhead. Experimental results demonstrate that ReToM improves FID and CLIP scores compared to baseline methods while maintaining comparable inference time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a moderately novel approach. Token merging itself is not new, but the specific combination of adaptive window sizes, local representative token selection based on cosine similarity, and similarity caching tailored for stable diffusion models is a unique contribution. The adaptive window size is conceptually simple but grounded in the structure of the U-Net architecture, lending it some merit.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the crucial problem of computational cost in diffusion models while improving, or at least maintaining, generation quality. The demonstrated improvements in FID and CLIP scores are important. By not requiring retraining/finetuning, ReToM presents a more practical drop-in solution for existing models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Improved Image Quality:**  The experimental results show improvements in image quality (FID and CLIP scores) compared to baseline models and other token merging techniques.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** ReToM claims comparable inference time.  While it introduces a similarity computation overhead, caching is used to mitigate this.
    *   **No Retraining Required:**  ReToM can be directly integrated into existing stable diffusion models without requiring fine-tuning.
    *   **Adaptive Window Size:** Adapting window size to different layers (particularly those within U-Net architecture) shows effectiveness in improvement.
    *   **Representative Token Selection:** Selection of representative token as the destination for merging shows performance boost over random.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are positive, the improvement margin is not dramatically large. The 6.2% improvement in FID, while meaningful, doesn't represent a breakthrough.
    *   **Similarity Computation Complexity:** Calculating cosine similarity, even with caching, adds computational overhead. The paper mentions computational cost is balanced out by the caching system, however the trade-off between the complexity added by calculating and caching similarity against reduced tokens count warrants further clarification.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** Although ImageNet is a good dataset, more diverse and challenging text prompts and a broader array of metrics (e.g., user studies for perceptual quality) would further strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While adaptive window sizes are mentioned, studies varying sizes and comparing performance among them are missing.

*   **Potential Influence:** ReToM has the potential to influence future research on efficient diffusion models. The ideas of adaptive windowing and similarity-based token selection are valuable contributions to the field and may be adopted and extended by other researchers. Its ease of integration is a plus.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper makes a solid contribution by addressing the computational bottleneck of stable diffusion models and enhancing image generation quality through a carefully designed token merging strategy. While the improvements are incremental and the experimental validation could be broader, the approach is promising and offers a practical solution for improving the efficiency of text-to-image generation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**  The score reflects the paper's decent but not groundbreaking novelty and its solid contribution to the efficiency of stable diffusion models. The improvements are measurable and valuable and the method's relative ease of implementation is a strong point. However, the improvements aren't transformative, there's room for further evaluation and ablation studies, and the complexity added by similarity calculation needs further consideration. The paper is a worthwhile addition to the field, but not a truly exceptional one.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Comprehensive Survey of Electronic Health Record Modeling: From Deep Learning Approaches to Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12774v1)
- **Authors**: Weijieying Ren, Jingxi Zhu, Zehao Liu, Tianxiang Zhao, Vasant Honavar
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence (AI) has demonstrated significant potential in transforming healthcare through the analysis and modeling of electronic health records (EHRs). However, the inherent heterogeneity, temporal irregularity, and domain-specific nature of EHR data present unique challenges that differ fundamentally from those in vision and natural language tasks. This survey offers a comprehensive overview of recent advancements at the intersection of deep learning, large language models (LLMs), and EHR modeling. We introduce a unified taxonomy that spans five key design dimensions: data-centric approaches, neural architecture design, learning-focused strategies, multimodal learning, and LLM-based modeling systems. Within each dimension, we review representative methods addressing data quality enhancement, structural and temporal representation, self-supervised learning, and integration with clinical knowledge. We further highlight emerging trends such as foundation models, LLM-driven clinical agents, and EHR-to-text translation for downstream reasoning. Finally, we discuss open challenges in benchmarking, explainability, clinical alignment, and generalization across diverse clinical settings. This survey aims to provide a structured roadmap for advancing AI-driven EHR modeling and clinical decision support. For a comprehensive list of EHR-related methods, kindly refer to https://survey-on-tabular-data.github.io/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper is a systematic survey of recent research on electronic health record (EHR) modeling, focusing on deep learning approaches integrated with large language models (LLMs). It presents a comprehensive overview of the field, structuring it around five key design dimensions: data-centric approaches, neural architecture design, learning-focused strategies, multimodal learning, and LLM-based modeling systems.  The survey reviews methods for data quality enhancement, structural and temporal representation, self-supervised learning, and clinical knowledge integration. It highlights emerging trends such as foundation models, LLM-driven clinical agents, and EHR-to-text translation for reasoning. The authors also address challenges in benchmarking, explainability, clinical alignment, and generalization. The survey's stated goal is to offer a roadmap for advancing AI-driven EHR modeling and clinical decision support. A companion website provides a comprehensive list of related methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The survey distinguishes itself from existing surveys in the EHR modeling domain by providing a *unified* and *comprehensive* view, encompassing data strategies, neural architectures, and LLM paradigms. Prior surveys tended to focus on isolated components like architectures or language models. The taxonomy presented is also claimed to be novel. However, many of the individual components and techniques surveyed are not new; the novelty lies in the systematic organization and synthesis.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a timely and relevant topic. The integration of deep learning and LLMs into EHR analysis has the potential to revolutionize healthcare. The survey provides a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners by organizing the rapidly growing literature in the field. The emphasis on emerging trends (foundation models, clinical agents) and open challenges adds practical value for directing future research efforts. The inclusion of a companion website for ongoing updates is a significant strength.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The survey seems to cover a wide range of relevant literature.
    *   **Clear Structure:** The organization around key design dimensions provides a helpful framework.
    *   **Timeliness:** It addresses a rapidly evolving field.
    *   **Practical Value:** The emphasis on emerging trends and open challenges makes it actionable.
    *   **Companion Website:**  Enhances the survey's long-term value and utility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of Critical Depth (potential):**  A survey's value depends on the depth of the critical analysis.  While the summary suggests a systematic approach, a critical evaluation needs to ensure the analysis goes beyond simply summarizing existing work and offers insightful comparative analysis.
    *   **Overclaim of Novelty:** It is possible that the claims of novelty regarding certain elements may be somewhat inflated.  The design choices *may* reflect a restructuring, synthesis, and focus on LLMs, but *could* also be considered incremental.
    *   **Potential Bias:**  Surveys can be influenced by the authors' perspective and expertise, potentially leading to a biased view of the field. This is not evident from the summary alone, but is a consideration in general for surveys.

*   **Potential Influence:** The survey has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   Providing a common language and framework for researchers.
    *   Highlighting promising research directions.
    *   Facilitating the development of more robust and clinically relevant EHR models.
    *   Promoting reproducibility and standardization of evaluation.

*Score: 7*

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a useful synthesis of current research in a highly active field, offering a novel structural approach. The potential to guide future research and standardize evaluation practices is substantial. However, a score above 7 would require more evidence of rigorous critical analysis *beyond* simply summarizing the literature. While the novel taxonomy is a plus, the value of *any* survey is dependent on its ability to provide insights through synthesis, comparison, and critical evaluation. If this element is strong within the whole paper, then the score would increase.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Compact Vision Transformer by Reduction of Kernel Complexity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12780v1)
- **Authors**: Yancheng Wang, Yingzhen Yang
- **Abstract**: Self-attention and transformer architectures have become foundational components in modern deep learning. Recent efforts have integrated transformer blocks into compact neural architectures for computer vision, giving rise to various efficient vision transformers. In this work, we introduce Transformer with Kernel Complexity Reduction, or KCR-Transformer, a compact transformer block equipped with differentiable channel selection, guided by a novel and sharp theoretical generalization bound. KCR-Transformer performs input/output channel selection in the MLP layers of transformer blocks to reduce the computational cost. Furthermore, we provide a rigorous theoretical analysis establishing a tight generalization bound for networks equipped with KCR-Transformer blocks. Leveraging such strong theoretical results, the channel pruning by KCR-Transformer is conducted in a generalization-aware manner, ensuring that the resulting network retains a provably small generalization error. Our KCR-Transformer is compatible with many popular and compact transformer networks, such as ViT and Swin, and it reduces the FLOPs of the vision transformers while maintaining or even improving the prediction accuracy. In the experiments, we replace all the transformer blocks in the vision transformers with KCR-Transformer blocks, leading to KCR-Transformer networks with different backbones. The resulting TCR-Transformers achieve superior performance on various computer vision tasks, achieving even better performance than the original models with even less FLOPs and parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel compact transformer block called KCR-Transformer (Kernel Complexity Reduction Transformer). It achieves efficiency by performing input/output channel selection in the MLP layers of transformer blocks based on a differentiable channel selection guided by a tight theoretical generalization bound. The KCR-Transformer aims to reduce the computational cost (FLOPs) while maintaining or even improving accuracy. The authors provide theoretical analysis establishing a generalization bound for KCR-Transformer equipped networks and demonstrate its compatibility with ViT and Swin transformer architectures.  Experimental results show KCR-Transformers achieve superior performance on image classification, object detection, and instance segmentation tasks, exceeding the performance of original models with reduced FLOPs and parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to transformer compression by reducing kernel complexity (KC) via channel pruning guided by a theoretically derived generalization bound.  The integration of kernel methods, specifically a truncated nuclear norm approximation, for channel selection is a relatively new idea.  Prior work often relied on empirical heuristics or spectral alignment of NTK, whereas this paper attempts to minimize an approximated KC directly. This aspect presents a clear novel contribution compared to existing methods. The approach to use a Nyström method to scale and approximate the kernel is a clever approach to address the heavy computational costs.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work lies in its potential to improve the efficiency of vision transformers without sacrificing accuracy.  The theoretical grounding of the channel pruning via the generalization bound provides a principled approach rather than relying on purely empirical techniques.  The compatibility with popular architectures like ViT and Swin enhances its potential impact and applicability.  However, the actual practical impact is dependent on the ease of implementation and tuning in diverse scenarios.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Foundation:**  A significant strength is the detailed theoretical analysis deriving a generalization bound based on kernel complexity. This allows for a more informed channel selection process.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results on various computer vision tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the KCR-Transformer.  The performance improvements with reduced FLOPs are promising.
    *   **Compatibility:**  The KCR-Transformer's modularity and compatibility with existing architectures like ViT and Swin contribute to its practical appeal.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost of Approximation:** While the Nyström method is used to *approximate* the computationally expensive calculations for kernel complexity, the approximation itself may still have a significant computational overhead, specifically regarding the eigendecomposition of W. It's not clear if this scales.
    *   **Complexity of Implementation:** The KCR-Transformer approach, involving a search phase and a retraining phase with a specific loss function, adds complexity to the training pipeline, which might be a barrier to adoption. The detailed tuning involved in the Algorithm 1 seems complex.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Scope:** While the theoretical analysis is a strong point, it still operates within certain assumptions. The sharpness of the bounds and the practical implications on different datasets might vary. The paper would benefit from a discussion of the limitations of the theory. It is crucial to understand the behavior of the network when the approximations fail.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a solid contribution with a good balance of theoretical analysis and empirical validation. The idea of reducing kernel complexity to guide channel pruning has merit, and the results are promising. However, there are practical concerns regarding the complexity of the method and the computational cost of approximating kernel complexity.

Score: 7.5
**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates a novel and theoretically-grounded approach for compressing vision transformers. The performance improvements on benchmark datasets are noteworthy. However, the practical adoption might be hindered by the complexity of implementation and tuning, and questions remain regarding the scalability and computational cost of kernel complexity approximations for very large models and datasets. Therefore, while promising, further investigation and simplification is warranted to significantly advance the field.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning Robust Negation Text Representations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12782v1)
- **Authors**: Thinh Hung Truong, Karin Verspoor, Trevor Cohn, Timothy Baldwin
- **Abstract**: Despite rapid adoption of autoregressive large language models, smaller text encoders still play an important role in text understanding tasks that require rich contextualized representations. Negation is an important semantic function that is still not properly captured by such methods, affecting many downstream applications relying on text embeddings. We propose a strategy to improve negation robustness of text encoders, by distilling data from large language models using diverse patterns of negation and hedging. We adopt a standard contrastive learning strategy to finetune a strong BERT-based model, and observe large improvement in negation understanding capabilities while maintaining competitive performance on general benchmarks. In addition, we also show that our method can be adapted to LLMs, leading to improved performance on negation benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of text encoders struggling with negation and hedging. It proposes a method to improve the robustness of text encoders to these linguistic phenomena by fine-tuning them on synthetic data generated by large language models (LLMs). The synthetic data, called HedgeTriple, is created using diverse patterns of negation and hedging based on linguistic taxonomies. The paper employs a contrastive learning strategy, showing significant improvements in negation understanding capabilities on various benchmarks while maintaining competitive performance on general tasks. Furthermore, the method is adapted to fine-tune decoder-only LLMs, leading to improvements in negation handling albeit with some performance degradation on arithmetic tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty in several aspects:
    *   **Taxonomy-driven data generation:** Unlike previous approaches that rely on simple rule-based negation or basic LLM prompting, this paper uses linguistically grounded taxonomies of negation and hedging to create more diverse and controlled synthetic data. This is a significant step towards addressing the limitations of existing negation augmentation techniques.
    *   **HedgeTriple dataset and contrastive fine-tuning:** The construction of the HedgeTriple dataset with explicit positive (hedged variants) and negative (negated variants) instances and the use of contrastive learning is a practical contribution.
    *   **Adaptation to LLMs:** Applying the technique to fine-tune decoder-only LLMs and observing the impact on both negation and general capabilities is a valuable extension.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve text understanding tasks where accurate handling of negation and hedging is crucial. Applications in areas like search (product features), scientific discourse analysis, and question answering (exclusion-type queries) could benefit. The method also serves as a practical approach for augmenting training data for specialized linguistic tasks. Furthermore, it showcases the utility of LLMs in distilling knowledge for smaller text encoders, a relevant trend in the current NLP landscape.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper presents thorough experiments demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method on several negation-focused benchmarks and general benchmark suite (MTEB).
    *   **Ablation studies:** Detailed ablation studies, including data attribution analysis and dataset size analysis, provide insights into the impact of different components of the approach (negation vs. hedging, quantity of data).
    *   **Error analysis:** The error analysis on GSM8K highlights the potential trade-offs between negation robustness and other capabilities, such as arithmetic reasoning, prompting further research.
    *   **Clear writing and organization:** The paper is well-written and organized, making the methodology and results easy to understand.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **English-centric:** The paper focuses exclusively on English, which limits the generalizability of the findings without further experimentation on other languages.
    *   **Potential for LLM hallucination in data generation:** Although the authors filter for topically similar sentences, the reliance on LLMs for synthetic data generation introduces a risk of hallucinated or factually incorrect data, which might inadvertently train the model on misleading signals, though they mention that they have upper thresholds for Levenshtein distance.
    *   **Limited evaluation of general capabilities:** Although the authors maintain competitive results on general benchmarks, a deeper evaluation of the impact on specific general tasks, particularly those sensitive to negation and hedging, would be beneficial.
    *   **No comparison to other contrastive learning methods:** The paper implements a common contrastive learning strategy. There are no experiments to demonstrate that this particular contrastive learning is a particularly strong contribution, rather than just applying any kind of contrastive learning to the data.
    *   **Degradation on GSM8K**: There are no experiments to improve the performance on arithmetic benchmarks, despite the degradation being identified. This somewhat limits the usability of the method, as negation is more widely applicable for general models than arithmetic.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The methodology provides a practical roadmap for improving negation and hedging understanding in text encoders using LLM-generated synthetic data.
    *   The HedgeTriple dataset could serve as a valuable resource for future research in this area.
    *   The analysis of the trade-offs between negation robustness and other capabilities could guide future research towards more balanced approaches.
    *   The investigation on taxonomy-driven data generation is helpful for guiding future data synthesis.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the combination of novelty, significance, and limitations, I assign the paper a score of **7**.

*   The work represents a significant advancement in handling negation and hedging compared to prior work, primarily due to the taxonomy-driven approach for creating diverse synthetic data.
*   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating consistent improvements on negation benchmarks.
*   However, the limitations mentioned (English-centric, LLM hallucination, limited evaluation of general capabilities, simple fine-tuning approach, degradation on benchmarks) prevent it from being a truly exceptional contribution. The LLM degradation is particularly concerning, as it introduces a trade-off that requires further investigation.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DeQA-Doc: Adapting DeQA-Score to Document Image Quality Assessment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12796v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Gao, Runze Liu, Yingzhe Peng, Shujian Yang, Jin Zhang, Kai Yang, Zhiyuan You
- **Abstract**: Document quality assessment is critical for a wide range of applications including document digitization, OCR, and archival. However, existing approaches often struggle to provide accurate and robust quality scores, limiting their applicability in practical scenarios. With the rapid progress in Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs), recent MLLM-based methods have achieved remarkable performance in image quality assessment. In this work, we extend this success to the document domain by adapting DeQA-Score, a state-of-the-art MLLM-based image quality scorer, for document quality assessment. We propose DeQA-Doc, a framework that leverages the visual language capabilities of MLLMs and a soft label strategy to regress continuous document quality scores. To adapt DeQA-Score to DeQA-Doc, we adopt two complementary solutions to construct soft labels without the variance information. Also, we relax the resolution constrains to support the large resolution of document images. Finally, we introduce ensemble methods to further enhance the performance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DeQA-Doc significantly outperforms existing baselines, offering accurate and generalizable document quality assessment across diverse degradation types. Codes and model weights are available in https://github.com/Junjie-Gao19/DeQA-Doc.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DeQA-Doc, an adaptation of the DeQA-Score framework (a multi-modal large language model-based image quality scorer) for document image quality assessment (DIQA). Recognizing the challenges of applying image quality models to the document domain, DeQA-Doc addresses issues like missing variance information in DIQA datasets (by proposing pseudo-variance and linear interpolation strategies for soft label construction) and the often higher resolution of document images (by removing absolute position embeddings or using a flexible resolution model like Qwen2.5-VL). The framework is trained using a hybrid loss, and performance is further enhanced through model and prompt ensembling. The authors demonstrate that DeQA-Doc outperforms existing DIQA baselines on the DIQA-5000 dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *adaptation* of an existing image quality model (DeQA-Score) to the document image domain. The proposed solutions for addressing the specific challenges of DIQA – namely, the lack of variance information for soft labels and accommodating higher-resolution images – are relatively straightforward. While these adaptations are pragmatic and effective, they don't introduce fundamentally new deep learning techniques. The use of ensembling is also a standard practice.

*   **Significance:** The significance comes from applying the capabilities of multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) to DIQA, which is still a relatively under-explored area compared to general image quality assessment. The performance gains demonstrated over existing DIQA baselines are meaningful, suggesting that MLLMs offer a viable path toward more robust and accurate document quality assessment. The paper provides a practical framework and identifies important considerations for applying MLLMs in this context. The readily available code and models will encourage further research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper identifies specific challenges associated with applying existing image quality models to the document domain.
    *   Effective adaptations: The proposed solutions for handling missing variance and high-resolution images are simple yet effective.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation: The paper includes ablation studies and ensemble experiments, providing a solid understanding of the framework's performance.
    *   Strong results: The method outperforms the baseline DIQA methods on a benchmark dataset.
    *   Availability of code and models: Enhances reproducibility and accelerates future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Incremental novelty: The core architectural components are derived from existing MLLM and IQA methods. The main contribution is adapting and tuning these components for document images.
    *   Limited dataset scope: The experiments are conducted on a single DIQA-5000 dataset, so the generalizability to other DIQA datasets with different characteristics is not fully established.
    *   Adaptations, while pragmatic, do not represent a significant breakthrough in deep learning techniques.
    *  Lack of a detailed comparison with existing DIQA systems employing techniques beyond basic handcrafted features and shallow models. Newer deep-learning-based methods, even if not MLLM-based, would give a more robust benchmark to compare against

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence the DIQA field by demonstrating the potential of MLLMs for this task. It provides a baseline and framework for future research to build upon. It might also encourage researchers to create DIQA datasets with more comprehensive annotations (including variance information) to better leverage the capabilities of MLLMs.

*   **Justification of Score:**  While the paper is technically sound, performs well empirically, and addresses a practical problem, its novelty is somewhat limited. The paper relies heavily on adapting existing techniques rather than introducing novel architectural components or training methodologies. Therefore, the score reflects this balance.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### MCPEval: Automatic MCP-based Deep Evaluation for AI Agent Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12806v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiwei Liu, Jielin Qiu, Shiyu Wang, Jianguo Zhang, Zuxin Liu, Roshan Ram, Haolin Chen, Weiran Yao, Huan Wang, Shelby Heinecke, Silvio Savarese, Caiming Xiong
- **Abstract**: The rapid rise of Large Language Models (LLMs)-based intelligent agents underscores the need for robust, scalable evaluation frameworks. Existing methods rely on static benchmarks and labor-intensive data collection, limiting practical assessment. We introduce \oursystemname, an open-source Model Context Protocol (MCP)-based framework that automates end-to-end task generation and deep evaluation of LLM agents across diverse domains. MCPEval standardizes metrics, seamlessly integrates with native agent tools, and eliminates manual effort in building evaluation pipelines. Empirical results across five real-world domains show its effectiveness in revealing nuanced, domain-specific performance. We publicly release MCPEval https://github.com/SalesforceAIResearch/MCPEval to promote reproducible and standardized LLM agent evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MCPEval: Automatic MCP-based Deep Evaluation for AI Agent Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MCPEval, an open-source framework for automatically evaluating AI agents that use Large Language Models (LLMs). MCPEval is built upon the Model Context Protocol (MCP), which standardizes interactions between LLMs and external systems. The framework automates the entire evaluation pipeline, including task generation, verification, model evaluation, and result analysis. It provides in-depth analysis, going beyond simple success/failure metrics to assess tool usage, planning, execution flow, and context awareness. The authors demonstrate its effectiveness across five real-world domains, showing its ability to reveal nuanced, domain-specific performance characteristics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a crucial need for scalable and robust evaluation frameworks for LLM-based agents. While existing methods exist, they often rely on static benchmarks or labor-intensive data collection. MCPEval's automation and deep integration with the MCP standard represent a significant step forward. The integration with MCP is not entirely new, as other frameworks like MCP-Radar and MCPWorld exist. However, MCPEval distinguishes itself by providing a fully automated evaluation process and a comprehensive deep analysis workflow.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to standardize and accelerate the evaluation process for LLM agents. This allows developers to quickly assess model capabilities, identify weaknesses, and optimize implementations. The open-source nature promotes reproducibility and collaboration within the research community. The revelation from experiments regarding the performance of small models suggests opportunities for cost-effective deployment without compromising efficiency.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Automation:** Eliminates manual bottlenecks in task creation and evaluation.
    *   **Deep Integration:** Leverages the MCP standard for seamless integration with native agent tools.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** Provides nuanced insights into agent behavior beyond simple success rates.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Released as an open-source platform.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** Evaluation conducted across diverse real-world domains.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Synthetic Data:** The evaluation relies entirely on synthetically generated data, which may not fully capture real-world complexities. This limits the generalizability of the results.
    *   **LLM-Based Judging:** Using LLM-based judges for evaluation can be computationally expensive and might introduce biases.
    *   **Potential Bias in Automated Verification:** Automated verification could produce false ground truth labels, particularly for ambiguous tasks.
    *   The reliance on comparing other models against GPT-4.1 to form the ground truth raises the question of how performance scores should be evaluated when other models diverge with distinct, creative approaches.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development and evaluation of LLM agents by promoting standardized evaluation practices and encouraging the development of more robust and capable systems. The actionable insights can be used to improve current implementations and guide future training strategies. It could lead to improved model context protocol tools in the future as well.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM agent evaluation. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, particularly given the practical relevance and the open-source nature of the project. However, the dependence on synthetic data and the limitations of LLM-based judging prevent a higher score. The contribution is incremental, advancing upon existing frameworks rather than revolutionizing the approach. Overall, the work advances the field of AI agent evaluation with a practical, useful, and reproducible methodology, warranting a score that reflects this progress.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models' Internal Perception of Symbolic Music
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12808v1)
- **Authors**: Andrew Shin, Kunitake Kaneko
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel at modeling relationships between strings in natural language and have shown promise in extending to other symbolic domains like coding or mathematics. However, the extent to which they implicitly model symbolic music remains underexplored. This paper investigates how LLMs represent musical concepts by generating symbolic music data from textual prompts describing combinations of genres and styles, and evaluating their utility through recognition and generation tasks. We produce a dataset of LLM-generated MIDI files without relying on explicit musical training. We then train neural networks entirely on this LLM-generated MIDI dataset and perform genre and style classification as well as melody completion, benchmarking their performance against established models. Our results demonstrate that LLMs can infer rudimentary musical structures and temporal relationships from text, highlighting both their potential to implicitly encode musical patterns and their limitations due to a lack of explicit musical context, shedding light on their generative capabilities for symbolic music.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs), trained solely on text, to implicitly model symbolic music. The study explores how LLMs represent musical concepts by generating MIDI files from textual prompts that describe genre and style combinations. These LLM-generated MIDI files are then used to train neural networks for genre/style classification and melody completion. The performance of these networks is benchmarked against existing models, demonstrating LLMs' potential to encode musical patterns and their limitations due to a lack of explicit musical context.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its direct examination of *unmodified* LLMs' ability to generate and understand structured symbolic music from *text alone*, without explicit musical training or pre-defined templates. This contrasts with previous works that often involve fine-tuning on music datasets or hybrid architectures.  The approach of using LLMs to generate training data and then evaluating downstream models is also a valid, though not groundbreaking, method. The focus on probing the *implicit* musical knowledge encoded in pre-trained LLMs sets it apart.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its exploration of LLMs as general-purpose pattern learners capable of cross-domain synthesis. If LLMs can model music concepts implicitly from text, it suggests a more profound level of abstraction and generalization than previously understood. This has implications for the broader field of AI creativity and interdisciplinary applications in music informatics. However, the achieved results are not ground-breaking and clearly demonstrates that LLMs lack human-like musical intelligence.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a well-defined experimental setup with clear research questions.
    *   The dataset creation process, although constrained, is thoroughly explained.
    *   The benchmark comparisons provide a solid basis for evaluating the performance of LLM-generated data.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations and proposes directions for future research.
    *   The paper clearly demonstrates the process with figures, allowing the audience to grasp the contents easily.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The limitations imposed on the generated music (fixed length, time signature, limited rhythmic values, specific instrumentation) significantly restrict the complexity and realism of the dataset, and inherently limit the performance of downstream models.
    *   The evaluation metrics (strict string matching for classification) are simplistic and may penalize musically reasonable classifications.
    *   The baseline models used for comparison (melody2vec, tonnetz) are not the state-of-the-art models for music classification and generation.
    *   The downstream neural network architecture (CNN and transformer) are quite simple compared to modern architectures tailored for music. This may not fully exploit the potential of the LLM-generated data.
    *   The paper relies on GPT-4 for data generation, but there's limited analysis of how different LLMs might affect the outcome.
    *   The "understanding" aspect is overstated.  The results primarily show pattern matching, not necessarily a deep musical understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could motivate further research into more sophisticated methods for leveraging LLMs in generative music systems. It highlights the importance of incorporating explicit musical knowledge or developing more advanced prompting strategies to improve the quality of LLM-generated music. The insights gained could also inform the design of LLMs with better cross-domain generalization capabilities.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to probing LLMs for implicit musical knowledge. However, the limitations in the generated data, evaluation metrics, and benchmark comparisons hinder the significance of the results. The paper establishes a baseline for future research, but falls short of making a truly transformative contribution to the field.
The models used in the experiment are also simple (e.g. vanilla CNN), so the evaluation results are not high, so its impacts on the field are limited.
Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### MCoT-RE: Multi-Faceted Chain-of-Thought and Re-Ranking for Training-Free Zero-Shot Composed Image Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12819v1)
- **Authors**: Jeong-Woo Park, Seong-Whan Lee
- **Abstract**: Composed Image Retrieval (CIR) is the task of retrieving a target image from a gallery using a composed query consisting of a reference image and a modification text. Among various CIR approaches, training-free zero-shot methods based on pre-trained models are cost-effective but still face notable limitations. For example, sequential VLM-LLM pipelines process each modality independently, which often results in information loss and limits cross-modal interaction. In contrast, methods based on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often focus exclusively on applying changes indicated by the text, without fully utilizing the contextual visual information from the reference image. To address these issues, we propose multi-faceted Chain-of-Thought with re-ranking (MCoT-RE), a training-free zero-shot CIR framework. MCoT-RE utilizes multi-faceted Chain-of-Thought to guide the MLLM to balance explicit modifications and contextual visual cues, generating two distinct captions: one focused on modification and the other integrating comprehensive visual-textual context. The first caption is used to filter candidate images. Subsequently, we combine these two captions and the reference image to perform multi-grained re-ranking. This two-stage approach facilitates precise retrieval by aligning with the textual modification instructions while preserving the visual context of the reference image. Through extensive experiments, MCoT-RE achieves state-of-the-art results among training-free methods, yielding improvements of up to 6.24% in Recall@10 on FashionIQ and 8.58% in Recall@1 on CIRR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MCoT-RE, a training-free zero-shot approach for Composed Image Retrieval (CIR).  MCoT-RE employs a multi-faceted Chain-of-Thought (MCoT) process to guide a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) to reason over both a reference image and text modification instructions.  The MCoT strategy generates two distinct captions: one focusing on the explicitly stated modifications and another integrating the broader visual context.  These captions are then used in a two-stage retrieval pipeline: first, filtering candidate images based on the modification-focused caption, and then re-ranking them using a combination of both captions and the reference image. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods on FashionIQ and CIRR datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the multi-faceted Chain-of-Thought prompting strategy and its use in generating two distinct captions tailored for different stages of retrieval. While using LLMs/MLLMs for CIR is not entirely new, the specific design of MCoT to explicitly capture both modifications and contextual information appears innovative. The two-stage retrieval using tailored captions is also a significant point of differentiation from single-caption approaches.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from improving zero-shot performance in a challenging task like CIR, especially without requiring task-specific training. Training-free methods are highly desirable due to their cost-effectiveness and adaptability. Achieving state-of-the-art results in this setting makes MCoT-RE a valuable contribution. The breakdown into distinct reasoning steps and caption generation also offer insights into how MLLMs can be better utilized for multimodal reasoning.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Performance:** The paper demonstrates clear quantitative improvements over existing training-free methods on standard benchmarks.
    * **Well-Defined Method:**  The MCoT-RE pipeline is clearly explained, with a detailed description of the prompting strategy, caption generation, and retrieval process. Algorithm 1 provides a concise summary of the steps.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are crucial for understanding the contribution of each component, demonstrating the necessity of both the two-stage approach and the combined caption strategy.
    * **Qualitative Results:** The example retrievals in Fig. 4 help to visualize the effectiveness of MCoT-RE in capturing both explicit and implicit visual cues.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on a Specific MLLM:** The paper relies on the Gemini 1.5 model. The performance and effectiveness of MCoT-RE might be influenced by the choice of MLLM. A more detailed discussion regarding the compatibility with different MLLM architectures could be added.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance may be sensitive to hyperparameters (α, β, k). While they mention empirically determining k, a more detailed analysis of the sensitivity and robustness of the system to variations in these hyperparameters would strengthen the work.
    * **Computational Cost:** While training-free, the inference costs using MLLMs can be substantial. The paper does not explicitly address the computational overhead of the proposed approach compared to other methods. It would be beneficial to discuss the inference time.
    * **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** While the qualitative results show successes, the paper would be strengthened by showing examples of failure cases and analyzing the reasons for failure.

* **Potential Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence the design of future zero-shot CIR methods, particularly in how MLLMs are prompted and utilized for multimodal reasoning. The idea of generating task-specific captions could be applicable to other multimodal tasks as well.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the reliance on a specific MLLM and some questions about hyperparameter sensitivity and computational cost, the clear improvement in performance, the well-defined method, the thorough ablation studies, and the valuable qualitative examples justify a high score.  The focus on leveraging both explicit and implicit cues with a carefully designed MCoT process is a significant contribution to the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Bridging the Gap: Leveraging Retrieval-Augmented Generation to Better Understand Public Concerns about Vaccines
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12840v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Javed, Sedigh Khademi Habibabadi, Christopher Palmer, Hazel Clothier, Jim Buttery, Gerardo Luis Dimaguila
- **Abstract**: Vaccine hesitancy threatens public health, leading to delayed or rejected vaccines. Social media is a vital source for understanding public concerns, and traditional methods like topic modelling often struggle to capture nuanced opinions. Though trained for query answering, large Language Models (LLMs) often miss current events and community concerns. Additionally, hallucinations in LLMs can compromise public health communication. To address these limitations, we developed a tool (VaxPulse Query Corner) using the Retrieval Augmented Generation technique. It addresses complex queries about public vaccine concerns on various online platforms, aiding public health administrators and stakeholders in understanding public concerns and implementing targeted interventions to boost vaccine confidence. Analysing 35,103 Shingrix social media posts, it achieved answer faithfulness (0.96) and relevance (0.94).
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "Bridging the Gap: Leveraging Retrieval-Augmented Generation to Better Understand Public Concerns about Vaccines."

**Summary**

The paper introduces VaxPulse Query Corner (VaxPulse QC), a tool designed to answer complex queries about public concerns related to vaccinations, using the Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) technique. The tool leverages online media data from various platforms (X, Reddit, YouTube, and Facebook) to understand public sentiment and address concerns about specific vaccines (in this case study, Shingrix).  The authors detail the data processing pipeline, including data scraping, preprocessing, segregation of vaccine-specific comments, embedding creation, retrieval, ranking, re-ranking, contextual compression, and answer formulation. The tool aims to bridge the gap left by traditional methods like topic modeling, which struggle with complex questions and nuanced opinions. The authors tested the system against a synthetic dataset and report on the performance of different components, specifically context retrieval and answer generation, showing improvements through a two-iteration process. The results show high faithfulness and relevancy scores for answer generation.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:*

While RAG itself isn't a novel technique, its application in vaccine hesitancy analysis, especially in a real-time, customizable query answering system like VaxPulse QC, exhibits some novelty. The incorporation of a two-stage retrieval and ranking approach and the use of contextual compression in the RAG pipeline shows efforts to optimize performance in this specific domain. To our knowledge, application of RAG method to the vaccination online discussions to address queries of public health administrators and other stakeholders is novel.  The use of RAGAS for automatic test case generation and system evaluation is another interesting choice that should be discussed further.

*Significance:*

The potential impact of this tool is significant, particularly for public health organizations struggling to understand and address vaccine hesitancy. By providing a means to quickly identify and respond to public concerns expressed online, VaxPulse QC could contribute to improved health communication and increased vaccine confidence. The system's ability to address specific queries, rather than relying on broad topic modeling, provides a more targeted approach to addressing misinformation and alleviating concerns.

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of vaccine hesitancy and the limitations of existing analytical methods.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The data processing pipeline is described in detail, making it easy to understand the different stages of the tool.
*   **Quantitative Evaluation:** The use of metrics like context precision, context recall, faithfulness, and relevancy allows for a quantitative assessment of the tool's performance.
*   **Real-World Application:**  The focus on a specific vaccine (Shingrix) provides a concrete example of how the tool can be used in practice.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:**  The system's evaluation relies on automatically generated test cases using RAGAS.  It would be more convincing to have evaluations performed by actual subject matter experts (public health officials) using real-world queries, to assess the system's utility in practice and validate its reliability.
*   **Dependency on Data Quality:** The accuracy and usefulness of the tool are heavily dependent on the quality and completeness of the data collected from online platforms.  The paper doesn't adequately address potential biases or limitations in the data itself.
*   **Lack of comparison:** Authors mention some implementations of RAG method but the current implementation is missing a comparison with them and also traditional systems like topic modelling.

*Justification for Score:*

The paper demonstrates a useful application of RAG in a domain with significant real-world impact. However, the evaluation is limited, and the system's reliance on data quality and the novelty of the approach is somewhat incremental. The system evaluation is performed using RAGAS which should be discussed further, comparing the generated test cases with test cases by human experts. It would be necessary to perform some statistical significance analysis.  Therefore, while the potential is there, further validation and a more thorough comparison with existing methods are needed to fully demonstrate the tool's effectiveness and superiority.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 6/10

### DEMONSTRATE: Zero-shot Language to Robotic Control via Multi-task Demonstration Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12855v1)
- **Authors**: Rahel Rickenbach, Bruce Lee, René Zurbrügg, Carmen Amo Alonso, Melanie N. Zeilinger
- **Abstract**: The integration of large language models (LLMs) with control systems has demonstrated significant potential in various settings, such as task completion with a robotic manipulator. A main reason for this success is the ability of LLMs to perform in-context learning, which, however, strongly relies on the design of task examples, closely related to the target tasks. Consequently, employing LLMs to formulate optimal control problems often requires task examples that contain explicit mathematical expressions, designed by trained engineers. Furthermore, there is often no principled way to evaluate for hallucination before task execution. To address these challenges, we propose DEMONSTRATE, a novel methodology that avoids the use of LLMs for complex optimization problem generations, and instead only relies on the embedding representations of task descriptions. To do this, we leverage tools from inverse optimal control to replace in-context prompt examples with task demonstrations, as well as the concept of multitask learning, which ensures target and example task similarity by construction. Given the fact that hardware demonstrations can easily be collected using teleoperation or guidance of the robot, our approach significantly reduces the reliance on engineering expertise for designing in-context examples. Furthermore, the enforced multitask structure enables learning from few demonstrations and assessment of hallucinations prior to task execution. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method through simulation and hardware experiments involving a robotic arm tasked with tabletop manipulation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DEMONSTRATE: Zero-shot Language to Robotic Control via Multi-task Demonstration Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DEMONSTRATE, a novel approach for robotic control using language commands. Unlike existing methods that heavily rely on LLMs to directly generate optimal control problems (OCPs) from natural language, DEMONSTRATE focuses on mapping language embeddings to OCP parameters learned from task demonstrations.
