### LoViC: Efficient Long Video Generation with Context Compression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12952v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxiu Jiang, Wenbo Li, Jingjing Ren, Yuping Qiu, Yong Guo, Xiaogang Xu, Han Wu, Wangmeng Zuo
- **Abstract**: Despite recent advances in diffusion transformers (DiTs) for text-to-video generation, scaling to long-duration content remains challenging due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. While prior efforts -- such as sparse attention and temporally autoregressive models -- offer partial relief, they often compromise temporal coherence or scalability. We introduce LoViC, a DiT-based framework trained on million-scale open-domain videos, designed to produce long, coherent videos through a segment-wise generation process. At the core of our approach is FlexFormer, an expressive autoencoder that jointly compresses video and text into unified latent representations. It supports variable-length inputs with linearly adjustable compression rates, enabled by a single query token design based on the Q-Former architecture. Additionally, by encoding temporal context through position-aware mechanisms, our model seamlessly supports prediction, retradiction, interpolation, and multi-shot generation within a unified paradigm. Extensive experiments across diverse tasks validate the effectiveness and versatility of our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LoViC, a novel framework for long video generation using context compression. LoViC addresses the computational challenges of scaling Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) to longer video sequences by employing a segment-wise generation process. A key component is FlexFormer, an autoencoder that compresses video and text into a unified latent representation using a single query token design and an interpolated positional encoding scheme (I-RoPE). This enables variable-length input support with adjustable compression rates.  LoViC supports various video generation tasks, including prediction, retrodiction, interpolation, and multi-shot generation within a unified framework. The model is trained on a million-scale open-domain video dataset, and extensive experiments validate its effectiveness and versatility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components. The FlexFormer architecture for context compression is a key contribution, particularly the single query token approach and I-RoPE for efficient encoding of long-range information. The unified framework for various video generation tasks is also noteworthy. The segment-wise approach isn't entirely novel, but the specific implementation using FlexFormer and its associated encoding methods adds a fresh perspective. The integration of both video and text compression within the same module is potentially innovative.

*   **Significance:** The limitations of self-attention in scaling DiTs to longer video sequences are well-established. LoViC offers a promising solution that could facilitate the generation of more coherent and extended video content. The method's ability to handle various video generation tasks within a single framework makes it more versatile than many existing approaches. The empirical results demonstrate competitive performance, particularly in maintaining temporal consistency. The framework will open avenues for researchers and practitioners to create longer and contextually rich videos more efficiently. A weakness of the experimental section is the limited number of baselines and lack of code availability which hinders independent verification of findings.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The FlexFormer architecture offers a viable solution to the computational cost of processing long video sequences.
    *   The paper demonstrates the capability to perform different video generation tasks with a unified framework.
    *   Experimental results showcase the effectiveness of LoViC in maintaining temporal consistency and visual coherence.
    *   The use of a million-scale open-domain dataset suggests the model's potential for real-world application.
    *   The paper is relatively well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the individual components are novel, the high-level segment-wise generation approach is not entirely new. The improvement is more incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   The baselines for comparison could be expanded. A more extensive comparison with recent state-of-the-art methods would further strengthen the paper. The inclusion of metrics focusing on compression efficiency would bolster the argument further.
    *   The novelty of some components (e.g., I-ROPE) needs more exhaustive analysis and ablation.
    *   Lack of code availability is a disadvantage, hindering reproducibility and wider adoption.
    *   The qualitative results lack fine-grained analysis, and there's not a great depth of discussion as to where the method fails.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the approach proves to be scalable and generalizable, it could influence future research in long-form video generation. The FlexFormer architecture could be adopted or adapted by others working in this area. The paper could potentially inspire the development of new methods that focus on context compression and efficient encoding of long-range dependencies.

**Justification of Score:**

LoViC addresses a significant problem in video generation and introduces interesting components like FlexFormer and I-ROPE. The results demonstrate improvements in temporal consistency. However, the approach builds upon existing techniques, and there is room for improvement in the experimental validation. Furthermore, the lack of code hampers the ability to reproduce and build upon the work. Based on this, the novelty is deemed moderately incremental. Hence, the score reflects a solid, but not exceptional, contribution to the field.

### FantasyPortrait: Enhancing Multi-Character Portrait Animation with Expression-Augmented Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12956v1)
- **Authors**: Qiang Wang, Mengchao Wang, Fan Jiang, Yaqi Fan, Yonggang Qi, Mu Xu
- **Abstract**: Producing expressive facial animations from static images is a challenging task. Prior methods relying on explicit geometric priors (e.g., facial landmarks or 3DMM) often suffer from artifacts in cross reenactment and struggle to capture subtle emotions. Furthermore, existing approaches lack support for multi-character animation, as driving features from different individuals frequently interfere with one another, complicating the task. To address these challenges, we propose FantasyPortrait, a diffusion transformer based framework capable of generating high-fidelity and emotion-rich animations for both single- and multi-character scenarios. Our method introduces an expression-augmented learning strategy that utilizes implicit representations to capture identity-agnostic facial dynamics, enhancing the model's ability to render fine-grained emotions. For multi-character control, we design a masked cross-attention mechanism that ensures independent yet coordinated expression generation, effectively preventing feature interference. To advance research in this area, we propose the Multi-Expr dataset and ExprBench, which are specifically designed datasets and benchmarks for training and evaluating multi-character portrait animations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FantasyPortrait significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both quantitative metrics and qualitative evaluations, excelling particularly in challenging cross reenactment and multi-character contexts. Our project page is https://fantasy-amap.github.io/fantasy-portrait/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "FantasyPortrait: Enhancing Multi-Character Portrait Animation with Expression-Augmented Diffusion Transformers" introduces a novel framework for generating high-fidelity, emotionally rich facial animations from static images, specifically targeting both single- and multi-character scenarios. The core contributions include: 1) an expression-augmented learning strategy that utilizes implicit expression representations to capture identity-agnostic facial dynamics and subtle emotions; 2) a masked cross-attention mechanism that facilitates synchronized multi-character animation while preventing feature interference between characters; and 3) the creation of ExprBench and Multi-Expr datasets for training and evaluating multi-character portrait animation models.  The authors demonstrate that their method, FantasyPortrait, outperforms state-of-the-art approaches, particularly in challenging cross-reenactment and multi-character contexts.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a gap:**  The paper tackles a relatively unexplored area: high-quality multi-character portrait animation with coordinated and independent expression control. Existing methods often focus on single-character scenarios or struggle with character interference in multi-character settings.
*   **Technical Novelty:** The expression-augmented learning strategy and the masked cross-attention mechanism are technically sound and appear to be effective in achieving their intended goals. The implicit expression representations are a good way to capture fine-grained expression features in a way that is identity-agnostic. The masked cross-attention is a smart way to coordinate animations between different characters, while ensuring that they each maintain their own identity.
*   **Dataset and Benchmark Contribution:**  The introduction of the Multi-Expr dataset and ExprBench benchmark fills a significant void in the field, providing valuable resources for future research and development in multi-character portrait animation. Creating and releasing datasets is always a positive contribution.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The paper presents comprehensive experimental results, including both quantitative metrics and qualitative evaluations, demonstrating the superiority of FantasyPortrait over existing methods.  The ablation studies offer valuable insights into the effectiveness of the individual components of the proposed framework.
*   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, clearly explaining the proposed method and its advantages.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Innovation:** While the individual components are well-engineered and integrated, it can be argued that they represent incremental improvements upon existing techniques (DiTs, attention mechanisms, implicit representation learning) rather than groundbreaking innovations.  The approach follows established trends in diffusion models and transformers.
*   **Computational Cost:** The paper mentions the use of 24 A100 GPUs for training, implying that the method may be computationally expensive and not easily accessible to researchers with limited resources. This is a general weakness for many works that use DiTs, but it is still important to consider.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** Although the authors claim strong generalization capabilities, the experiments primarily focus on specific types of portraits (realistic human styles and anthropomorphic styles). It is unclear how well FantasyPortrait would perform on more diverse or stylized character designs.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** The paper briefly touches on the potential for misuse, but a more in-depth discussion of the ethical implications of high-fidelity portrait animation technology would be beneficial.
*  **Lack of Failure Case Analysis:** Although the paper presents a thorough evaluation of the proposed method, there is no discussion of specific failure cases and/or error analysis.

**Significance:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of portrait animation by addressing the challenge of generating high-quality, emotionally expressive animations in both single- and multi-character scenarios. The proposed framework and the accompanying datasets and benchmarks have the potential to accelerate research and development in this area. However, the incremental nature of the technical contributions and the computational cost may limit its immediate impact.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

FantasyPortrait presents a valuable advancement in portrait animation, effectively tackling the complexities of multi-character control and emotion expression. The novel expression-augmented learning and masked cross-attention mechanisms are well-designed and demonstrably improve performance compared to existing methods. The addition of a specific dataset for multi-character scenarios and the benchmark ExprBench significantly support the field, driving progress on this niche but important problem. However, the approach relies on existing technologies (DiTs, attention mechanisms), so it does not represent a paradigm shift in the field, but more a very well-engineered step forward. The high computational cost to train also limits the immediate impact of the approach, though it is not an inherent flaw in the model itself. A more in-depth exploration of failure modes and ethical considerations would be beneficial. For this reason, a score of 7.5 is warranted. It is not an earth-shattering breakthrough, but it is a solid piece of research with important practical implications.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### RGB Pre-Training Enhanced Unobservable Feature Latent Diffusion Model for Spectral Reconstruction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12967v1)
- **Authors**: Keli Deng, Jie Nie, Yuntao Qian
- **Abstract**: Spectral reconstruction (SR) is a crucial problem in image processing that requires reconstructing hyperspectral images (HSIs) from the corresponding RGB images. A key difficulty in SR is estimating the unobservable feature, which encapsulates significant spectral information not captured by RGB imaging sensors. The solution lies in effectively constructing the spectral-spatial joint distribution conditioned on the RGB image to complement the unobservable feature. Since HSIs share a similar spatial structure with the corresponding RGB images, it is rational to capitalize on the rich spatial knowledge in RGB pre-trained models for spectral-spatial joint distribution learning. To this end, we extend the RGB pre-trained latent diffusion model (RGB-LDM) to an unobservable feature LDM (ULDM) for SR. As the RGB-LDM and its corresponding spatial autoencoder (SpaAE) already excel in spatial knowledge, the ULDM can focus on modeling spectral structure. Moreover, separating the unobservable feature from the HSI reduces the redundant spectral information and empowers the ULDM to learn the joint distribution in a compact latent space. Specifically, we propose a two-stage pipeline consisting of spectral structure representation learning and spectral-spatial joint distribution learning to transform the RGB-LDM into the ULDM. In the first stage, a spectral unobservable feature autoencoder (SpeUAE) is trained to extract and compress the unobservable feature into a 3D manifold aligned with RGB space. In the second stage, the spectral and spatial structures are sequentially encoded by the SpeUAE and the SpaAE, respectively. The ULDM is then acquired to model the distribution of the coded unobservable feature with guidance from the corresponding RGB images. Experimental results on SR and downstream relighting tasks demonstrate that our proposed method achieves state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel spectral reconstruction (SR) method called Unobservable Feature Latent Diffusion Model (ULDM) designed to reconstruct hyperspectral images (HSIs) from RGB images. The key idea is to leverage the spatial knowledge already present in pre-trained RGB latent diffusion models (RGB-LDM) to assist in estimating the "unobservable feature" – the spectral information missing in RGB images. The method consists of a two-stage training pipeline: 1) spectral structure representation learning using a Spectral Unobservable Feature Autoencoder (SpeUAE), and 2) spectral-spatial joint distribution learning using a fine-tuned RGB-LDM, now operating as a ULDM.  Experiments on three datasets (CAVE, ICVL, NTIRE22) and a downstream relighting task demonstrate state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has some novelty in adapting a pre-trained diffusion model for spectral reconstruction by focusing on the unobservable feature. The two-stage training pipeline and use of the SpeUAE to align the spectral representation with the RGB space are also novel contributions. The idea of explicitly modeling and separating the unobservable spectral information offers a structured approach that differs from directly mapping RGB to HSI.

*   **Significance:** Spectral reconstruction from RGB is an important and challenging problem.  The reported state-of-the-art results on standard datasets, along with the improved performance on the relighting task, suggest that this method makes a significant contribution to the field.  The use of pre-trained models can potentially alleviate the need for large amounts of RGB-HSI training data, addressing a practical limitation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated approach based on the limitations of existing methods and the benefits of leveraging pre-trained models.
    *   Clearly defined two-stage training pipeline with specific objectives.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on multiple datasets and a downstream task.
    *   Quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate superior performance.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components.
    *   Addresses the limitation of limited training data required for diffusion models using pre-trained models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on a pre-trained RGB-LDM, potentially introducing biases based on the training data used for the RGB-LDM. While the paper argues for leveraging existing spatial knowledge, it's not clear how to handle scenarios where the spatial characteristics of the target HSI differ significantly from the RGB-LDM's training data.
    *   Although the paper addresses the computational cost by using only 20 inference steps, the overall complexity of the training pipeline (two stages, autoencoders, diffusion models) could be a limitation for some applications.
    *   While the mathematical formulation is provided, a more intuitive explanation of why separating the unobservable feature *specifically* leads to improved performance could strengthen the paper.
    *   The dependence on the pre-trained RGB-LDM might hinder adaptability to other imaging modalities or domains where such a pre-trained model isn't available.
*   **Clarity of writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow. The figures are helpful in understanding the proposed method.

*   **Potential impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in spectral reconstruction and hyperspectral imaging by demonstrating the effectiveness of leveraging pre-trained models and focusing on unobservable features. The relighting application also shows the practical benefits of the method. Further investigation is needed to understand the generalizability of the approach to different scenes and illuminant conditions.

**Overall Score and Justification**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to spectral reconstruction that leverages pre-trained diffusion models. While there are limitations related to the reliance on the RGB-LDM and potential biases, the reported results are compelling and demonstrate a significant improvement over existing methods. The two-stage training pipeline and the idea of explicitly modeling the unobservable feature are valuable contributions to the field. The paper is well-written and the experiments are comprehensive. This makes the paper valuable. I rate it an 7 because while strong, the dependence on pre-trained RGB models and lack of a comprehensive robustness analysis limit it's widespread applicability.

### Non-differentiable Reward Optimization for Diffusion-based Autonomous Motion Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12977v1)
- **Authors**: Giwon Lee, Daehee Park, Jaewoo Jeong, Kuk-Jin Yoon
- **Abstract**: Safe and effective motion planning is crucial for autonomous robots. Diffusion models excel at capturing complex agent interactions, a fundamental aspect of decision-making in dynamic environments. Recent studies have successfully applied diffusion models to motion planning, demonstrating their competence in handling complex scenarios and accurately predicting multi-modal future trajectories. Despite their effectiveness, diffusion models have limitations in training objectives, as they approximate data distributions rather than explicitly capturing the underlying decision-making dynamics. However, the crux of motion planning lies in non-differentiable downstream objectives, such as safety (collision avoidance) and effectiveness (goal-reaching), which conventional learning algorithms cannot directly optimize. In this paper, we propose a reinforcement learning-based training scheme for diffusion motion planning models, enabling them to effectively learn non-differentiable objectives that explicitly measure safety and effectiveness. Specifically, we introduce a reward-weighted dynamic thresholding algorithm to shape a dense reward signal, facilitating more effective training and outperforming models trained with differentiable objectives. State-of-the-art performance on pedestrian datasets (CrowdNav, ETH-UCY) compared to various baselines demonstrates the versatility of our approach for safe and effective motion planning.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary of the paper and then conduct a rigorous and critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, followed by an assigned score and justification.

**Paper Summary:**

The paper "Non-differentiable Reward Optimization for Diffusion-based Autonomous Motion Planning" addresses the limitation of diffusion models in motion planning, which primarily focus on reconstructing training data distributions rather than explicitly optimizing non-differentiable downstream objectives like safety (collision avoidance) and effectiveness (goal reaching).  The authors propose a reinforcement learning (RL)-based training scheme to enable diffusion motion planning models to learn these objectives directly.  They introduce a reward-weighted dynamic thresholding algorithm to shape a dense reward signal, addressing the sparse reward problem common in RL and promoting more effective training. The results show state-of-the-art performance on pedestrian datasets (CrowdNav, ETH-UCY) compared to various baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core novelty lies in the integration of reinforcement learning techniques, specifically reward shaping with dynamic thresholding, into the training process of diffusion models for motion planning. While both diffusion models and RL have been used in motion planning before, combining them in this specific way to directly optimize non-differentiable objectives is relatively new. Existing works mostly focus on using diffusion models for trajectory generation or prediction and then applying differentiable loss functions for training.

*   **Strength:** The explicit optimization of non-differentiable rewards is a significant step forward.  Traditional methods are limited by the need for differentiable approximations, which can lead to suboptimal performance.
*   **Weakness:** While the combination is novel, the individual components (diffusion models, RL, reward shaping) are well-established. The key innovation is in *how* these components are combined and used.  The dynamic thresholding algorithm, while effective, is a relatively simple approach to address the sparse reward problem.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** This research has the potential to significantly improve the safety and effectiveness of autonomous motion planning systems. Explicitly training for collision avoidance and goal reaching is crucial for real-world deployment. The improvements demonstrated on standard datasets are a promising indicator.
*   **Strength:** Making diffusion models sensitive to downstream objectives is an important area. Diffusion models can model complex multi-modal behaviors which would be valuable in many motion planning applications.
*   **Weakness:** The reliance on a predefined reward function is a potential limitation. Defining appropriate rewards for complex scenarios can be challenging and may require careful tuning. The performance is also tied to the quality of the underlying diffusion model architecture; this work mainly focuses on the training procedure rather than architectural innovation. The method's performance gains are demonstrated primarily on pedestrian navigation datasets, and it needs further study on the robustness and scalability to other complex systems like autonomous driving.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence future research by:

*   Encouraging the use of RL-based training schemes for diffusion models in other domains where non-differentiable objectives are important.
*   Inspiring the development of more sophisticated reward shaping techniques for diffusion-based motion planning.
*   Leading to more robust and reliable autonomous motion planning systems that can handle complex real-world scenarios.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   The paper tackles a significant problem in the field of diffusion-based motion planning.
*   The proposed approach is novel in its integration of RL and diffusion models for explicit optimization of non-differentiable rewards.
*   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the method on standard datasets.
*   The paper has the potential to influence future research and development in the field.
*   However, the individual components of the approach are not entirely new, and the reliance on a predefined reward function is a limitation. Also, the paper needs to provide more details about the method's robustness and scalability to other systems, e.g., autonomous driving.
